People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, yeah—thanks. So, I guess the main thing is at work. I process insurance claims, and I keep getting up from my desk during the day even when I know I’m supposed to stay there and finish reviewing them. It’s kind of embarrassing because it’s not like I don’t know what I’m supposed to be doing.

Like, I’ll stand up to get water or just… walk over somewhere, and then I’m thinking about something totally unrelated, like whether I paid my phone bill or what I’m making for dinner, and then I realize I’ve been away from my workstation again. But yeah, the big reason I came in is that I can’t seem to stay put long enough to get through my claim reviews the way I’m expected to.

Um… outside of work, I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I notice I get kind of scattered sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s the same thing or just normal life stuff.

Like, at home I’ll start doing one thing and then end up thinking about, I don’t know, whether I need to buy groceries, and then somehow I’m looking at my kitchen cabinets because one of the hinges is loose—actually I’ve been meaning to fix that for weeks. But I guess I haven’t really tracked it the way I have at work, because work is where people notice and there are actual deadlines and stuff.

So… maybe? I’m not sure. What do you mean exactly by outside of my job?

Yeah… I think so, looking back. It’s funny because I wouldn’t have said that before, like I didn’t think of myself as a “problem kid” or anything. And my mom kept some of those report cards in this big folder—she keeps everything, like warranty cards from appliances we haven’t had in years—but looking back, yeah, it seems like it was showing up even in elementary school.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks consistently throughout your childhood and adolescence?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… looking back, it does seem like it was pretty consistent, at least with the focus and finishing stuff. It wasn’t like it suddenly started now.\n\nIn elementary school my teachers would say I was bright, which I remember because my mom really liked that part, but then the same comments would say I lost worksheets, or missed instructions, or needed reminders to finish classwork. She kept some of those old report cards in this folder—she keeps everything, honestly—but yeah, those kinds of notes were there early on.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever interrupted others or blurted out answers before they finished speaking?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / insurance_claims_or_policy_processing_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.   },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I was known for that, exactly? Like, I’m sure I’ve accidentally talked over people before—everyone does sometimes—but I don’t have a clear memory of teachers constantly saying I blurted out answers or anything.

I did sometimes answer too fast if I thought I knew what someone was asking, but then again, my uncle does that all the time at family dinners and everyone just kind of talks over each other, so it’s hard to tell what’s “interrupting” versus just… how people talk. But yeah, I wouldn’t say that was a big complaint I heard about me.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say often. I mean, I probably do it sometimes, especially if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure it’s a pattern people complain about.

Finishing sentences… maybe occasionally with people I’m close to? Like my sister and I do that, but she also does it back, so I don’t know if that counts or if it’s just conversation. At work I try pretty hard not to, because in claims calls and stuff you kind of have to let people finish explaining, even if they’re taking the long way around.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as super impulsive, like doing wild things without thinking. But then, I guess it depends what you mean by “act on impulse.”

Like, if you mean buying something randomly because I saw it online… I mean, I’ve done that, but also who hasn’t? My cousin sends me these deals all the time and then suddenly I’m looking at headphones I don’t even need. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly making huge decisions without thinking them through.

So… maybe sometimes with small stuff, but I don’t know if it’s often or really a major problem.

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, in school I remember organization being kind of hard, but it’s clearer to me now at work, honestly. Like since my caseload got bigger, my claim folders and notes and email flags just kind of end up all over the place. I’ll have one thing half-marked in the system, then a sticky note, then an email I flagged but forgot why I flagged it.

And then I spend too much time trying to figure out which policy review I’m supposed to tackle first instead of just doing it. It reminds me of school a little, like when I’d have different notebooks or folders and somehow still not know what was due first. But yeah—compared to other people, I guess it did seem like I had to work harder to keep things in order.

I was more the kid teachers said was smart, but then on report cards they’d write stuff like I lost worksheets, or I didn’t follow the instructions, or I needed reminders to finish classwork.\n\nMy mom actually kept some of those old comments in a folder somewhere—she keeps everything, like even old appliance manuals from microwaves we don’t own anymore, which is… anyway. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I ran around sometimes, but I don’t remember being, like, the kid who was always climbing on furniture or getting yelled at for being too rough.\n\nMy brother was more that kind of kid—he broke this lamp once trying to jump from the couch to the chair, and somehow I got blamed at first because I was in the room, which still annoys me a little. And my mom kept some of those report cards in this big folder—she keeps everything, like warranty cards from appliances we haven’t had in years—but looking back, yeah, it seems like it was showing up even in elementary school.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks consistently throughout your childhood and adolescence?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… looking back, it does seem like it was pretty consistent, at least with the focus and finishing stuff. She kept some of those old report cards in this folder—she keeps everything, honestly—but yeah, those kinds of notes were there early on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others or blurted out answers before they finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Predominantly Inattentive Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or finishing their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say often. I mean, I probably do it sometimes, especially if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure it’s a pattern people complain about.\n\nFinishing sentences… maybe occasionally with people I’m close to? Like my sister and I do that, but she also does it back, so I don’t know if that counts or if it’s just conversation. At work I try pretty hard not to, because in claims calls and stuff you kind of have to let people finish explaining, even if they’re taking the long way around. Which, honestly, I also take the long way around sometimes—like right now.\n\nSo… not something I’ve really noticed as a big issue, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as super impulsive, like doing wild things without thinking. My cousin sends me these deals all the time and then suddenly I’m looking at headphones I don’t even need. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… outside of work is harder to say, because I kind of measure everything by work lately. Like even when I’m home, I’m thinking about the files I didn’t finish, or whether I missed some detail in a policy note.

The big issue right now is my supervisor actually put me on performance coaching because my claim files are falling behind and they’re questioning my accuracy. Which feels… embarrassing, honestly. I was trying to get onto the senior adjuster track, and now I’m worried I’m messing that up or could lose the chance completely.

So daily life-wise, yeah, it’s spilling over because I’m stressed about it even when I’m not there. But the concrete problem is mostly work.

But yeah, the big reason I came in is that I can’t seem to stay put long enough to get through my claim reviews the way I’m expected to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted by your surroundings or thoughts while you're working?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I try to look like I’m paying attention, but then I notice my foot’s been going the whole time.\n\nIt’s funny, the chairs in our conference room are really uncomfortable too, so I used to blame that, but it’s not just that. I mean, I notice I get kind of scattered sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s the same thing or just normal life stuff.\n\nLike, at home I’ll start doing one thing and then end up thinking about, I don’t know, whether I need to buy groceries, and then somehow I’m looking at my kitchen cabinets because one of the hinges is loose—actually I’ve been meaning to fix that for weeks. But I guess I haven’t really tracked it the way I have at work, because work is where people notice and there are actual deadlines and stuff.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m not sure. What do you mean exactly by outside of my job?\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, looking back. It’s funny because I wouldn’t have said that before, like I didn’t think of myself as a “problem kid” or anything. I was more the kid teachers said was smart, but then on report cards they’d write stuff like I lost worksheets, or I didn’t follow the instructions, or I needed reminders to finish classwork.\n\nMy mom actually kept some of those old comments in a folder somewhere—she keeps everything, like even old appliance manuals from microwaves we don’t own anymore, which is… anyway. But yeah, it wasn’t brand new as an adult. In elementary school there were definitely notes about me missing what the teacher said or not finishing unless someone came by and reminded me.\n\nDoctor: Did you often get into trouble for running, climbing, or playing too rough when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I ran around sometimes, but I don’t remember being, like, the kid who was always climbing on furniture or getting yelled at for being too rough.\n\nMy brother was more that kind of kid—he broke this lamp once trying to jump from the couch to the chair, and somehow I got blamed at first because I was in the room, which still annoys me a little. But for me specifically… I don’t think that was a big thing? I’d have to ask my mom if there were stories I’m forgetting.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more trouble following rules or completing tasks compared to your peers when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… at least with completing tasks. And my mom kept some of those report cards in this big folder—she keeps everything, like warranty cards from appliances we haven’t had in years—but looking back, yeah, it seems like it was showing up even in elementary school.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks consistently throughout your childhood and adolescence?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… looking back, it does seem like it was pretty consistent, at least with the focus and finishing stuff. I don’t think I was known for that, exactly? It’s frustrating because it’s not hard material exactly, it’s just like my attention slides off it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Like in trainings or those longer team meetings, I’m usually doing something—bouncing my leg, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair. I try to look like I’m paying attention, but then I notice my foot’s been going the whole time.\n\nIt’s funny, the chairs in our conference room are really uncomfortable too, so I used to blame that, but it’s not just that. But I guess I haven’t really tracked it the way I have at work, because work is where people notice and there are actual deadlines and stuff.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m not sure. What do you mean exactly by outside of my job?\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, looking back. It’s funny because I wouldn’t have said that before, like I didn’t think of myself as a “problem kid” or anything. I was more the kid teachers said was smart, but then on report cards they’d write stuff like I lost worksheets, or I didn’t follow the instructions, or I needed reminders to finish classwork.\n\nMy mom actually kept some of those old comments in a folder somewhere—she keeps everything, like even old appliance manuals from microwaves we don’t own anymore, which is… anyway. But yeah, it wasn’t brand new as an adult. In elementary school there were definitely notes about me missing what the teacher said or not finishing unless someone came by and reminded me.\n\nDoctor: Did you often get into trouble for running, climbing, or playing too rough when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I ran around sometimes, but I don’t remember being, like, the kid who was always climbing on furniture or getting yelled at for being too rough.\n\nMy brother was more that kind of kid—he broke this lamp once trying to jump from the couch to the chair, and somehow I got blamed at first because I was in the room, which still annoys me a little. But for me specifically… I don’t think that was a big thing? I’d have to ask my mom if there were stories I’m forgetting.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more trouble following rules or completing tasks compared to your peers when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… at least with completing tasks. Not so much like I was constantly breaking rules on purpose or anything, but teachers would write that I was bright and then, you know, I’d lose worksheets or miss part of the instructions and need someone to remind me to finish classwork.\n\nIt’s weird, because at the time I just thought everyone got those comments sometimes. And my mom kept some of those report cards in this big folder—she keeps everything, like warranty cards from appliances we haven’t had in years—but looking back, yeah, it seems like it was showing up even in elementary school.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks consistently throughout your childhood and adolescence?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… looking back, it does seem like it was pretty consistent, at least with the focus and finishing stuff. It wasn’t like it suddenly started now.\n\nIn elementary school my teachers would say I was bright, which I remember because my mom really liked that part, but then the same comments would say I lost worksheets, or missed instructions, or needed reminders to finish classwork. She kept some of those old report cards in this folder—she keeps everything, honestly—but yeah, those kinds of notes were there early on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others or blurted out answers before they finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I was known for that, exactly? Like, I’m sure I’ve accidentally talked over people before—everyone does sometimes—but I don’t have a clear memory of teachers constantly saying I blurted out answers or anything.\n\nI did sometimes answer too fast if I thought I knew what someone was asking, but then again, my uncle does that all the time at family dinners and everyone just kind of talks over each other, so it’s hard to tell what’s “interrupting” versus just… how people talk. But yeah, I wouldn’t say that was a big complaint I heard about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or finishing their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say often. I mean, I probably do it sometimes, especially if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure it’s a pattern people complain about.\n\nFinishing sentences… maybe occasionally with people I’m close to? Like my sister and I do that, but she also does it back, so I don’t know if that counts or if it’s just conversation. At work I try pretty hard not to, because in claims calls and stuff you kind of have to let people finish explaining, even if they’re taking the long way around. Which, honestly, I also take the long way around sometimes—like right now.\n\nSo… not something I’ve really noticed as a big issue, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Like, I’m sure I’ve accidentally talked over people before—everyone does sometimes—but I don’t have a clear memory of teachers constantly saying I blurted out answers or anything.\n\nI did sometimes answer too fast if I thought I knew what someone was asking, but then again, my uncle does that all the time at family dinners and everyone just kind of talks over each other, so it’s hard to tell what’s “interrupting” versus just… how people talk. But yeah, I wouldn’t say that was a big complaint I heard about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or finishing their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say often. I mean, I probably do it sometimes, especially if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure it’s a pattern people complain about.\n\nFinishing sentences… maybe occasionally with people I’m close to? Like my sister and I do that, but she also does it back, so I don’t know if that counts or if it’s just conversation. At work I try pretty hard not to, because in claims calls and stuff you kind of have to let people finish explaining, even if they’re taking the long way around. Which, honestly, I also take the long way around sometimes—like right now.\n\nSo… not something I’ve really noticed as a big issue, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. But yeah—compared to other people, I guess it did seem like I had to work harder to keep things in order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks are causing problems in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… outside of work is harder to say, because I kind of measure everything by work lately. Which feels… embarrassing, honestly. But the concrete problem is mostly work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a strong need to be active and doing something, like you can't just relax and be still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I don’t know if I’d say I need to be active like I’m going out running around or anything, but sitting still can be weirdly hard.\n\nLike in trainings or longer team meetings, I’ll catch myself bouncing my leg the whole time, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair. It’s kind of embarrassing because it’s not like I don’t know what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nLike, I’ll stand up to get water or just… walk over somewhere, and then I’m thinking about something totally unrelated, like whether I paid my phone bill or what I’m making for dinner, and then I realize I’ve been away from my workstation again. Like, if people near me start talking about another claim, even if it’s not mine, my brain just kind of… turns toward that instead of what I’m reading. It’s frustrating because it’s not hard material exactly, it’s just like my attention slides off it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Like in trainings or those longer team meetings, I’m usually doing something—bouncing my leg, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair. What do you mean exactly by outside of my job?\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, looking back. I was more the kid teachers said was smart, but then on report cards they’d write stuff like I lost worksheets, or I didn’t follow the instructions, or I needed reminders to finish classwork.\n\nMy mom actually kept some of those old comments in a folder somewhere—she keeps everything, like even old appliance manuals from microwaves we don’t own anymore, which is… anyway. But yeah, it wasn’t brand new as an adult. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I ran around sometimes, but I don’t remember being, like, the kid who was always climbing on furniture or getting yelled at for being too rough.\n\nMy brother was more that kind of kid—he broke this lamp once trying to jump from the couch to the chair, and somehow I got blamed at first because I was in the room, which still annoys me a little. But for me specifically… I don’t think that was a big thing? I’d have to ask my mom if there were stories I’m forgetting.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more trouble following rules or completing tasks compared to your peers when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… at least with completing tasks. Not so much like I was constantly breaking rules on purpose or anything, but teachers would write that I was bright and then, you know, I’d lose worksheets or miss part of the instructions and need someone to remind me to finish classwork.\n\nIt’s weird, because at the time I just thought everyone got those comments sometimes. And my mom kept some of those report cards in this big folder—she keeps everything, like warranty cards from appliances we haven’t had in years—but looking back, yeah, it seems like it was showing up even in elementary school.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks consistently throughout your childhood and adolescence?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… looking back, it does seem like it was pretty consistent, at least with the focus and finishing stuff. It wasn’t like it suddenly started now.\n\nIn elementary school my teachers would say I was bright, which I remember because my mom really liked that part, but then the same comments would say I lost worksheets, or missed instructions, or needed reminders to finish classwork. She kept some of those old report cards in this folder—she keeps everything, honestly—but yeah, those kinds of notes were there early on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others or blurted out answers before they finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I was known for that, exactly? Like, I’m sure I’ve accidentally talked over people before—everyone does sometimes—but I don’t have a clear memory of teachers constantly saying I blurted out answers or anything.\n\nI did sometimes answer too fast if I thought I knew what someone was asking, but then again, my uncle does that all the time at family dinners and everyone just kind of talks over each other, so it’s hard to tell what’s “interrupting” versus just… how people talk. But yeah, I wouldn’t say that was a big complaint I heard about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or finishing their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say often. I mean, I probably do it sometimes, especially if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure it’s a pattern people complain about.\n\nFinishing sentences… maybe occasionally with people I’m close to? Like my sister and I do that, but she also does it back, so I don’t know if that counts or if it’s just conversation. At work I try pretty hard not to, because in claims calls and stuff you kind of have to let people finish explaining, even if they’re taking the long way around. Which, honestly, I also take the long way around sometimes—like right now.\n\nSo… not something I’ve really noticed as a big issue, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as super impulsive, like doing wild things without thinking. But then, I guess it depends what you mean by “act on impulse.”\n\nLike, if you mean buying something randomly because I saw it online… I mean, I’ve done that, but also who hasn’t? My cousin sends me these deals all the time and then suddenly I’m looking at headphones I don’t even need. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly making huge decisions without thinking them through.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes with small stuff, but I don’t know if it’s often or really a major problem.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more trouble with organization and time management compared to your peers when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, in school I remember organization being kind of hard, but it’s clearer to me now at work, honestly. Like since my caseload got bigger, my claim folders and notes and email flags just kind of end up all over the place. I’ll have one thing half-marked in the system, then a sticky note, then an email I flagged but forgot why I flagged it.\n\nAnd then I spend too much time trying to figure out which policy review I’m supposed to tackle first instead of just doing it. It reminds me of school a little, like when I’d have different notebooks or folders and somehow still not know what was due first. But yeah—compared to other people, I guess it did seem like I had to work harder to keep things in order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks are causing problems in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… outside of work is harder to say, because I kind of measure everything by work lately. Like even when I’m home, I’m thinking about the files I didn’t finish, or whether I missed some detail in a policy note.\n\nThe big issue right now is my supervisor actually put me on performance coaching because my claim files are falling behind and they’re questioning my accuracy. Which feels… embarrassing, honestly. I was trying to get onto the senior adjuster track, and now I’m worried I’m messing that up or could lose the chance completely.\n\nSo daily life-wise, yeah, it’s spilling over because I’m stressed about it even when I’m not there. But the concrete problem is mostly work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a strong need to be active and doing something, like you can't just relax and be still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I don’t know if I’d say I need to be active like I’m going out running around or anything, but sitting still can be weirdly hard.\n\nLike in trainings or longer team meetings, I’ll catch myself bouncing my leg the whole time, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair. Sometimes I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention, especially if my supervisor’s there, but then I realize I’ve been tapping for like ten minutes. And my mom kept some of those report cards in this big folder—she keeps everything, like warranty cards from appliances we haven’t had in years—but looking back, yeah, it seems like it was showing up even in elementary school.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks consistently throughout your childhood and adolescence?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… looking back, it does seem like it was pretty consistent, at least with the focus and finishing stuff. It wasn’t like it suddenly started now.\n\nIn elementary school my teachers would say I was bright, which I remember because my mom really liked that part, but then the same comments would say I lost worksheets, or missed instructions, or needed reminders to finish classwork. She kept some of those old report cards in this folder—she keeps everything, honestly—but yeah, those kinds of notes were there early on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others or blurted out answers before they finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I was known for that, exactly? Like, I’m sure I’ve accidentally talked over people before—everyone does sometimes—but I don’t have a clear memory of teachers constantly saying I blurted out answers or anything.\n\nI did sometimes answer too fast if I thought I knew what someone was asking, but then again, my uncle does that all the time at family dinners and everyone just kind of talks over each other, so it’s hard to tell what’s “interrupting” versus just… how people talk. But yeah, I wouldn’t say that was a big complaint I heard about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or finishing their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say often. I mean, I probably do it sometimes, especially if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure it’s a pattern people complain about.\n\nFinishing sentences… maybe occasionally with people I’m close to? Like my sister and I do that, but she also does it back, so I don’t know if that counts or if it’s just conversation. At work I try pretty hard not to, because in claims calls and stuff you kind of have to let people finish explaining, even if they’re taking the long way around. Which, honestly, I also take the long way around sometimes—like right now.\n\nSo… not something I’ve really noticed as a big issue, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as super impulsive, like doing wild things without thinking. But then, I guess it depends what you mean by “act on impulse.”\n\nLike, if you mean buying something randomly because I saw it online… I mean, I’ve done that, but also who hasn’t? My cousin sends me these deals all the time and then suddenly I’m looking at headphones I don’t even need. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly making huge decisions without thinking them through.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes with small stuff, but I don’t know if it’s often or really a major problem.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more trouble with organization and time management compared to your peers when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, in school I remember organization being kind of hard, but it’s clearer to me now at work, honestly. Like since my caseload got bigger, my claim folders and notes and email flags just kind of end up all over the place. I’ll have one thing half-marked in the system, then a sticky note, then an email I flagged but forgot why I flagged it.\n\nAnd then I spend too much time trying to figure out which policy review I’m supposed to tackle first instead of just doing it. It reminds me of school a little, like when I’d have different notebooks or folders and somehow still not know what was due first. But yeah—compared to other people, I guess it did seem like I had to work harder to keep things in order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks are causing problems in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… outside of work is harder to say, because I kind of measure everything by work lately. Like even when I’m home, I’m thinking about the files I didn’t finish, or whether I missed some detail in a policy note.\n\nThe big issue right now is my supervisor actually put me on performance coaching because my claim files are falling behind and they’re questioning my accuracy. Which feels… embarrassing, honestly. I was trying to get onto the senior adjuster track, and now I’m worried I’m messing that up or could lose the chance completely.\n\nSo daily life-wise, yeah, it’s spilling over because I’m stressed about it even when I’m not there. But the concrete problem is mostly work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a strong need to be active and doing something, like you can't just relax and be still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I don’t know if I’d say I need to be active like I’m going out running around or anything, but sitting still can be weirdly hard.\n\nLike in trainings or longer team meetings, I’ll catch myself bouncing my leg the whole time, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair. Sometimes I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention, especially if my supervisor’s there, but then I realize I’ve been tapping for like ten minutes. It’s not even that I’m trying to be annoying, it just kind of happens.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I wouldn’t say people are like… sitting me down and saying I’m ruining the relationship or anything.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I’m not as present as I should be, but then I start thinking about, like, whether that’s just normal stress or me overthinking it. Like my brother will text me about some random family plan, and then I’ll be thinking about an email I forgot to send, and then suddenly it’s two days later—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by “caused problems.”\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a clear relationship problem the way I have at work. It’s more fuzzy, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus or restlessness in multiple settings, such as at home, with friends, or in other activities outside of work and school?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. Like, at home I do notice I’ll start doing one thing and then somehow end up opening my phone or thinking about something else, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing or just… normal.\n\nWith friends, I guess I’m usually okay? I mean, sometimes I’ll drift a little if we’re just sitting around talking, but then again everybody checks out sometimes. It’s harder for me to point to a clear example outside of work or school and say, “Yes, this is definitely the same problem.”\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe, but I’m not really sure. Work is where it’s obvious to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s harder for me to point to a clear example outside of work or school and say, “Yes, this is definitely the same problem.”\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe, but I’m not really sure. Work is where it’s obvious to me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you experience difficulties with focus or restlessness before the age of 12?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / insurance_claims_or_policy_processing_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Looking back, this wasn’t brand new for me; in elementary school my teachers used to write that I was bright but lost worksheets, missed instructions, and needed reminders to finish classwork.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it wasn’t like this suddenly started when I got older.

In elementary school, I remember teachers writing stuff like I was bright, but I’d lose worksheets or miss instructions. My mom kept some of those old report cards in this folder somewhere—actually she keeps everything, like receipts from 2008 for no reason—but yeah, the comments were kind of the same theme. Like I needed reminders to finish classwork, or I’d be sitting there not done because I hadn’t followed what we were supposed to do.

At the time I don’t think anyone made a huge deal out of it, because I wasn’t trying to cause trouble or anything. But yeah, before 12, I think it was there.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.1, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah—thanks. So, I guess the main thing is at work. I process insurance claims, and I keep getting up from my desk during the day even when I know I’m supposed to stay there and finish reviewing them. It’s kind of embarrassing because it’s not like I don’t know what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nLike, I’ll stand up to get water or just… walk over somewhere, and then I’m thinking about something totally unrelated, like whether I paid my phone bill or what I’m making for dinner, and then I realize I’ve been away from my workstation again. But yeah, the big reason I came in is that I can’t seem to stay put long enough to get through my claim reviews the way I’m expected to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted by your surroundings or thoughts while you're working?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like, if people near me start talking about another claim, even if it’s not mine, my brain just kind of… turns toward that instead of what I’m reading. Or I’ll suddenly remember some random errand, like needing to pick up laundry detergent or something, and then I’m gone from the claim in front of me.\n\nThen I look back and realize I didn’t take in any of that section, so I have to reread the same paragraph a few times. It’s frustrating because it’s not hard material exactly, it’s just like my attention slides off it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Like in trainings or those longer team meetings, I’m usually doing something—bouncing my leg, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair. I try to look like I’m paying attention, but then I notice my foot’s been going the whole time.\n\nIt’s funny, the chairs in our conference room are really uncomfortable too, so I used to blame that, but it’s not just that. I just kind of can’t sit completely still for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness outside of your job as well?\n\nPatient: Um… outside of work, I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I notice I get kind of scattered sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s the same thing or just normal life stuff.\n\nLike, at home I’ll start doing one thing and then end up thinking about, I don’t know, whether I need to buy groceries, and then somehow I’m looking at my kitchen cabinets because one of the hinges is loose—actually I’ve been meaning to fix that for weeks. But I guess I haven’t really tracked it the way I have at work, because work is where people notice and there are actual deadlines and stuff.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m not sure. What do you mean exactly by outside of my job?\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, looking back. It’s funny because I wouldn’t have said that before, like I didn’t think of myself as a “problem kid” or anything. I was more the kid teachers said was smart, but then on report cards they’d write stuff like I lost worksheets, or I didn’t follow the instructions, or I needed reminders to finish classwork.\n\nMy mom actually kept some of those old comments in a folder somewhere—she keeps everything, like even old appliance manuals from microwaves we don’t own anymore, which is… anyway. But yeah, it wasn’t brand new as an adult. In elementary school there were definitely notes about me missing what the teacher said or not finishing unless someone came by and reminded me.\n\nDoctor: Did you often get into trouble for running, climbing, or playing too rough when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I ran around sometimes, but I don’t remember being, like, the kid who was always climbing on furniture or getting yelled at for being too rough.\n\nMy brother was more that kind of kid—he broke this lamp once trying to jump from the couch to the chair, and somehow I got blamed at first because I was in the room, which still annoys me a little. But for me specifically… I don’t think that was a big thing? I’d have to ask my mom if there were stories I’m forgetting.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more trouble following rules or completing tasks compared to your peers when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… at least with completing tasks. Not so much like I was constantly breaking rules on purpose or anything, but teachers would write that I was bright and then, you know, I’d lose worksheets or miss part of the instructions and need someone to remind me to finish classwork.\n\nIt’s weird, because at the time I just thought everyone got those comments sometimes. And my mom kept some of those report cards in this big folder—she keeps everything, like warranty cards from appliances we haven’t had in years—but looking back, yeah, it seems like it was showing up even in elementary school.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks consistently throughout your childhood and adolescence?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… looking back, it does seem like it was pretty consistent, at least with the focus and finishing stuff. It wasn’t like it suddenly started now.\n\nIn elementary school my teachers would say I was bright, which I remember because my mom really liked that part, but then the same comments would say I lost worksheets, or missed instructions, or needed reminders to finish classwork. She kept some of those old report cards in this folder—she keeps everything, honestly—but yeah, those kinds of notes were there early on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others or blurted out answers before they finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I was known for that, exactly? Like, I’m sure I’ve accidentally talked over people before—everyone does sometimes—but I don’t have a clear memory of teachers constantly saying I blurted out answers or anything.\n\nI did sometimes answer too fast if I thought I knew what someone was asking, but then again, my uncle does that all the time at family dinners and everyone just kind of talks over each other, so it’s hard to tell what’s “interrupting” versus just… how people talk. But yeah, I wouldn’t say that was a big complaint I heard about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or finishing their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say often. I mean, I probably do it sometimes, especially if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure it’s a pattern people complain about.\n\nFinishing sentences… maybe occasionally with people I’m close to? Like my sister and I do that, but she also does it back, so I don’t know if that counts or if it’s just conversation. At work I try pretty hard not to, because in claims calls and stuff you kind of have to let people finish explaining, even if they’re taking the long way around. Which, honestly, I also take the long way around sometimes—like right now.\n\nSo… not something I’ve really noticed as a big issue, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as super impulsive, like doing wild things without thinking. But then, I guess it depends what you mean by “act on impulse.”\n\nLike, if you mean buying something randomly because I saw it online… I mean, I’ve done that, but also who hasn’t? My cousin sends me these deals all the time and then suddenly I’m looking at headphones I don’t even need. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly making huge decisions without thinking them through.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes with small stuff, but I don’t know if it’s often or really a major problem.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more trouble with organization and time management compared to your peers when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, in school I remember organization being kind of hard, but it’s clearer to me now at work, honestly. Like since my caseload got bigger, my claim folders and notes and email flags just kind of end up all over the place. I’ll have one thing half-marked in the system, then a sticky note, then an email I flagged but forgot why I flagged it.\n\nAnd then I spend too much time trying to figure out which policy review I’m supposed to tackle first instead of just doing it. It reminds me of school a little, like when I’d have different notebooks or folders and somehow still not know what was due first. But yeah—compared to other people, I guess it did seem like I had to work harder to keep things in order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks are causing problems in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… outside of work is harder to say, because I kind of measure everything by work lately. Like even when I’m home, I’m thinking about the files I didn’t finish, or whether I missed some detail in a policy note.\n\nThe big issue right now is my supervisor actually put me on performance coaching because my claim files are falling behind and they’re questioning my accuracy. Which feels… embarrassing, honestly. I was trying to get onto the senior adjuster track, and now I’m worried I’m messing that up or could lose the chance completely.\n\nSo daily life-wise, yeah, it’s spilling over because I’m stressed about it even when I’m not there. But the concrete problem is mostly work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a strong need to be active and doing something, like you can't just relax and be still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I don’t know if I’d say I need to be active like I’m going out running around or anything, but sitting still can be weirdly hard.\n\nLike in trainings or longer team meetings, I’ll catch myself bouncing my leg the whole time, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair. Sometimes I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention, especially if my supervisor’s there, but then I realize I’ve been tapping for like ten minutes. It’s not even that I’m trying to be annoying, it just kind of happens.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I wouldn’t say people are like… sitting me down and saying I’m ruining the relationship or anything.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I’m not as present as I should be, but then I start thinking about, like, whether that’s just normal stress or me overthinking it. Like my brother will text me about some random family plan, and then I’ll be thinking about an email I forgot to send, and then suddenly it’s two days later—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by “caused problems.”\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a clear relationship problem the way I have at work. It’s more fuzzy, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus or restlessness in multiple settings, such as at home, with friends, or in other activities outside of work and school?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. Like, at home I do notice I’ll start doing one thing and then somehow end up opening my phone or thinking about something else, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing or just… normal.\n\nWith friends, I guess I’m usually okay? I mean, sometimes I’ll drift a little if we’re just sitting around talking, but then again everybody checks out sometimes. It’s harder for me to point to a clear example outside of work or school and say, “Yes, this is definitely the same problem.”\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe, but I’m not really sure. Work is where it’s obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus or restlessness before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it wasn’t like this suddenly started when I got older.\n\nIn elementary school, I remember teachers writing stuff like I was bright, but I’d lose worksheets or miss instructions. Like I needed reminders to finish classwork, or I’d be sitting there not done because I hadn’t followed what we were supposed to do.\n\nAt the time I don’t think anyone made a huge deal out of it, because I wasn’t trying to cause trouble or anything. But yeah, before 12, I think it was there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not sure I can really say it’s the same level outside of work. Like, work gives me a really obvious structure to compare against—deadlines, emails, claims, all that stuff—so it’s easier to notice when I’m slipping.

At home it’s more… vague. I’ll have moments where I’m like, “Was I just spacing out?” but then I’ll get distracted thinking about whether that counts, or whether everyone does that after a long day. And outside of work, like with errands or just hanging out, I don’t know that I’ve tracked it closely enough.

So I guess I don’t have a clear answer. It probably shows up sometimes, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it continuing at the same level outside of work.

I just kind of can’t sit completely still for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness outside of your job as well?\n\nPatient: Um… outside of work, I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I notice I get kind of scattered sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s the same thing or just normal life stuff.\n\nLike, at home I’ll start doing one thing and then end up thinking about, I don’t know, whether I need to buy groceries, and then somehow I’m looking at my kitchen cabinets because one of the hinges is loose—actually I’ve been meaning to fix that for weeks. But I guess I haven’t really tracked it the way I have at work, because work is where people notice and there are actual deadlines and stuff.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m not sure. What do you mean exactly by outside of my job?\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, looking back. It’s funny because I wouldn’t have said that before, like I didn’t think of myself as a “problem kid” or anything. I was more the kid teachers said was smart, but then on report cards they’d write stuff like I lost worksheets, or I didn’t follow the instructions, or I needed reminders to finish classwork.\n\nMy mom actually kept some of those old comments in a folder somewhere—she keeps everything, like even old appliance manuals from microwaves we don’t own anymore, which is… anyway. But yeah, it wasn’t brand new as an adult. In elementary school there were definitely notes about me missing what the teacher said or not finishing unless someone came by and reminded me.\n\nDoctor: Did you often get into trouble for running, climbing, or playing too rough when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I ran around sometimes, but I don’t remember being, like, the kid who was always climbing on furniture or getting yelled at for being too rough.\n\nMy brother was more that kind of kid—he broke this lamp once trying to jump from the couch to the chair, and somehow I got blamed at first because I was in the room, which still annoys me a little. But for me specifically… I don’t think that was a big thing? I’d have to ask my mom if there were stories I’m forgetting.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more trouble following rules or completing tasks compared to your peers when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… at least with completing tasks. Not so much like I was constantly breaking rules on purpose or anything, but teachers would write that I was bright and then, you know, I’d lose worksheets or miss part of the instructions and need someone to remind me to finish classwork.\n\nIt’s weird, because at the time I just thought everyone got those comments sometimes. And my mom kept some of those report cards in this big folder—she keeps everything, like warranty cards from appliances we haven’t had in years—but looking back, yeah, it seems like it was showing up even in elementary school.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks consistently throughout your childhood and adolescence?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… looking back, it does seem like it was pretty consistent, at least with the focus and finishing stuff. It wasn’t like it suddenly started now.\n\nIn elementary school my teachers would say I was bright, which I remember because my mom really liked that part, but then the same comments would say I lost worksheets, or missed instructions, or needed reminders to finish classwork. She kept some of those old report cards in this folder—she keeps everything, honestly—but yeah, those kinds of notes were there early on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others or blurted out answers before they finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I was known for that, exactly? Like, I’m sure I’ve accidentally talked over people before—everyone does sometimes—but I don’t have a clear memory of teachers constantly saying I blurted out answers or anything.\n\nI did sometimes answer too fast if I thought I knew what someone was asking, but then again, my uncle does that all the time at family dinners and everyone just kind of talks over each other, so it’s hard to tell what’s “interrupting” versus just… how people talk. But yeah, I wouldn’t say that was a big complaint I heard about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or finishing their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say often. I mean, I probably do it sometimes, especially if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure it’s a pattern people complain about.\n\nFinishing sentences… maybe occasionally with people I’m close to? Like my sister and I do that, but she also does it back, so I don’t know if that counts or if it’s just conversation. At work I try pretty hard not to, because in claims calls and stuff you kind of have to let people finish explaining, even if they’re taking the long way around. Which, honestly, I also take the long way around sometimes—like right now.\n\nSo… not something I’ve really noticed as a big issue, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as super impulsive, like doing wild things without thinking. But then, I guess it depends what you mean by “act on impulse.”\n\nLike, if you mean buying something randomly because I saw it online… I mean, I’ve done that, but also who hasn’t? My cousin sends me these deals all the time and then suddenly I’m looking at headphones I don’t even need. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly making huge decisions without thinking them through.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes with small stuff, but I don’t know if it’s often or really a major problem.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more trouble with organization and time management compared to your peers when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, in school I remember organization being kind of hard, but it’s clearer to me now at work, honestly. Like since my caseload got bigger, my claim folders and notes and email flags just kind of end up all over the place. I’ll have one thing half-marked in the system, then a sticky note, then an email I flagged but forgot why I flagged it.\n\nAnd then I spend too much time trying to figure out which policy review I’m supposed to tackle first instead of just doing it. It reminds me of school a little, like when I’d have different notebooks or folders and somehow still not know what was due first. But yeah—compared to other people, I guess it did seem like I had to work harder to keep things in order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks are causing problems in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… outside of work is harder to say, because I kind of measure everything by work lately. Like even when I’m home, I’m thinking about the files I didn’t finish, or whether I missed some detail in a policy note.\n\nThe big issue right now is my supervisor actually put me on performance coaching because my claim files are falling behind and they’re questioning my accuracy. Which feels… embarrassing, honestly. I was trying to get onto the senior adjuster track, and now I’m worried I’m messing that up or could lose the chance completely.\n\nSo daily life-wise, yeah, it’s spilling over because I’m stressed about it even when I’m not there. But the concrete problem is mostly work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a strong need to be active and doing something, like you can't just relax and be still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I don’t know if I’d say I need to be active like I’m going out running around or anything, but sitting still can be weirdly hard.\n\nLike in trainings or longer team meetings, I’ll catch myself bouncing my leg the whole time, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair. Sometimes I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention, especially if my supervisor’s there, but then I realize I’ve been tapping for like ten minutes. It’s not even that I’m trying to be annoying, it just kind of happens.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I wouldn’t say people are like… sitting me down and saying I’m ruining the relationship or anything.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I’m not as present as I should be, but then I start thinking about, like, whether that’s just normal stress or me overthinking it. Like my brother will text me about some random family plan, and then I’ll be thinking about an email I forgot to send, and then suddenly it’s two days later—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by “caused problems.”\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a clear relationship problem the way I have at work. It’s more fuzzy, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus or restlessness in multiple settings, such as at home, with friends, or in other activities outside of work and school?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. Like, at home I do notice I’ll start doing one thing and then somehow end up opening my phone or thinking about something else, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing or just… normal.\n\nWith friends, I guess I’m usually okay? I mean, sometimes I’ll drift a little if we’re just sitting around talking, but then again everybody checks out sometimes. It’s harder for me to point to a clear example outside of work or school and say, “Yes, this is definitely the same problem.”\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe, but I’m not really sure. Work is where it’s obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus or restlessness before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it wasn’t like this suddenly started when I got older.\n\nIn elementary school, I remember teachers writing stuff like I was bright, but I’d lose worksheets or miss instructions. My mom kept some of those old report cards in this folder somewhere—actually she keeps everything, like receipts from 2008 for no reason—but yeah, the comments were kind of the same theme. Like I needed reminders to finish classwork, or I’d be sitting there not done because I hadn’t followed what we were supposed to do.\n\nAt the time I don’t think anyone made a huge deal out of it, because I wasn’t trying to cause trouble or anything. But yeah, before 12, I think it was there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these difficulties with focus and restlessness continuing at the same level into your adult life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can really say it’s the same level outside of work. Like, work gives me a really obvious structure to compare against—deadlines, emails, claims, all that stuff—so it’s easier to notice when I’m slipping.\n\nAt home it’s more… vague. I’ll have moments where I’m like, “Was I just spacing out?” but then I’ll get distracted thinking about whether that counts, or whether everyone does that after a long day. And outside of work, like with errands or just hanging out, I don’t know that I’ve tracked it closely enough.\n\nSo I guess I don’t have a clear answer. But for me specifically… I don’t think that was a big thing? I’d have to ask my mom if there were stories I’m forgetting.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more trouble following rules or completing tasks compared to your peers when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… at least with completing tasks. Not so much like I was constantly breaking rules on purpose or anything, but teachers would write that I was bright and then, you know, I’d lose worksheets or miss part of the instructions and need someone to remind me to finish classwork.\n\nIt’s weird, because at the time I just thought everyone got those comments sometimes. And my mom kept some of those report cards in this big folder—she keeps everything, like warranty cards from appliances we haven’t had in years—but looking back, yeah, it seems like it was showing up even in elementary school.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks consistently throughout your childhood and adolescence?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… looking back, it does seem like it was pretty consistent, at least with the focus and finishing stuff. It wasn’t like it suddenly started now.\n\nIn elementary school my teachers would say I was bright, which I remember because my mom really liked that part, but then the same comments would say I lost worksheets, or missed instructions, or needed reminders to finish classwork. She kept some of those old report cards in this folder—she keeps everything, honestly—but yeah, those kinds of notes were there early on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others or blurted out answers before they finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I was known for that, exactly? Like, I’m sure I’ve accidentally talked over people before—everyone does sometimes—but I don’t have a clear memory of teachers constantly saying I blurted out answers or anything.\n\nI did sometimes answer too fast if I thought I knew what someone was asking, but then again, my uncle does that all the time at family dinners and everyone just kind of talks over each other, so it’s hard to tell what’s “interrupting” versus just… how people talk. But yeah, I wouldn’t say that was a big complaint I heard about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or finishing their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say often. I mean, I probably do it sometimes, especially if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure it’s a pattern people complain about.\n\nFinishing sentences… maybe occasionally with people I’m close to? Like my sister and I do that, but she also does it back, so I don’t know if that counts or if it’s just conversation. At work I try pretty hard not to, because in claims calls and stuff you kind of have to let people finish explaining, even if they’re taking the long way around. Which, honestly, I also take the long way around sometimes—like right now.\n\nSo… not something I’ve really noticed as a big issue, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as super impulsive, like doing wild things without thinking. But then, I guess it depends what you mean by “act on impulse.”\n\nLike, if you mean buying something randomly because I saw it online… I mean, I’ve done that, but also who hasn’t? My cousin sends me these deals all the time and then suddenly I’m looking at headphones I don’t even need. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly making huge decisions without thinking them through.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes with small stuff, but I don’t know if it’s often or really a major problem.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more trouble with organization and time management compared to your peers when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, in school I remember organization being kind of hard, but it’s clearer to me now at work, honestly. Like since my caseload got bigger, my claim folders and notes and email flags just kind of end up all over the place. I’ll have one thing half-marked in the system, then a sticky note, then an email I flagged but forgot why I flagged it.\n\nAnd then I spend too much time trying to figure out which policy review I’m supposed to tackle first instead of just doing it. It reminds me of school a little, like when I’d have different notebooks or folders and somehow still not know what was due first. But yeah—compared to other people, I guess it did seem like I had to work harder to keep things in order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks are causing problems in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… outside of work is harder to say, because I kind of measure everything by work lately. Like even when I’m home, I’m thinking about the files I didn’t finish, or whether I missed some detail in a policy note.\n\nThe big issue right now is my supervisor actually put me on performance coaching because my claim files are falling behind and they’re questioning my accuracy. Which feels… embarrassing, honestly. I was trying to get onto the senior adjuster track, and now I’m worried I’m messing that up or could lose the chance completely.\n\nSo daily life-wise, yeah, it’s spilling over because I’m stressed about it even when I’m not there. But the concrete problem is mostly work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a strong need to be active and doing something, like you can't just relax and be still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I don’t know if I’d say I need to be active like I’m going out running around or anything, but sitting still can be weirdly hard.\n\nLike in trainings or longer team meetings, I’ll catch myself bouncing my leg the whole time, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair. Sometimes I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention, especially if my supervisor’s there, but then I realize I’ve been tapping for like ten minutes. It’s not even that I’m trying to be annoying, it just kind of happens.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or completing tasks caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I wouldn’t say people are like… sitting me down and saying I’m ruining the relationship or anything.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I’m not as present as I should be, but then I start thinking about, like, whether that’s just normal stress or me overthinking it. Like my brother will text me about some random family plan, and then I’ll be thinking about an email I forgot to send, and then suddenly it’s two days later—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by “caused problems.”\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a clear relationship problem the way I have at work. It’s more fuzzy, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus or restlessness in multiple settings, such as at home, with friends, or in other activities outside of work and school?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. Like, at home I do notice I’ll start doing one thing and then somehow end up opening my phone or thinking about something else, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing or just… normal.\n\nWith friends, I guess I’m usually okay? I mean, sometimes I’ll drift a little if we’re just sitting around talking, but then again everybody checks out sometimes. It’s harder for me to point to a clear example outside of work or school and say, “Yes, this is definitely the same problem.”\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe, but I’m not really sure. Work is where it’s obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus or restlessness before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, it wasn’t like this suddenly started when I got older.\n\nIn elementary school, I remember teachers writing stuff like I was bright, but I’d lose worksheets or miss instructions. My mom kept some of those old report cards in this folder somewhere—actually she keeps everything, like receipts from 2008 for no reason—but yeah, the comments were kind of the same theme. Like I needed reminders to finish classwork, or I’d be sitting there not done because I hadn’t followed what we were supposed to do.\n\nAt the time I don’t think anyone made a huge deal out of it, because I wasn’t trying to cause trouble or anything. But yeah, before 12, I think it was there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these difficulties with focus and restlessness continuing at the same level into your adult life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can really say it’s the same level outside of work. Like, work gives me a really obvious structure to compare against—deadlines, emails, claims, all that stuff—so it’s easier to notice when I’m slipping.\n\nAt home it’s more… vague. I’ll have moments where I’m like, “Was I just spacing out?” but then I’ll get distracted thinking about whether that counts, or whether everyone does that after a long day. And outside of work, like with errands or just hanging out, I don’t know that I’ve tracked it closely enough.\n\nSo I guess I don’t have a clear answer. It probably shows up sometimes, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it continuing at the same level outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things on impulse without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, not in a big way that stands out to me.\n\nI might do small stuff without thinking, like buy a snack I didn’t need or click on something and then realize I’ve been looking at random reviews for, like, kitchen knives when I don’t even cook that much. Actually my roommate has this one knife that’s weirdly nice, and he’s very intense about it, like he sharpens it with this whole stone thing—\n\nSorry, what was the question exactly? Impulse, right. Yeah, I guess I don’t really see myself as someone who does major impulsive things without thinking them through. At least nothing comes to mind right away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
